To use them, we will retain the skeleton vertices whenever possible and replace obstructed segments by short exterior routes. A size restriction guarantees the finite lifts needed for this construction; the endpoints must also lie outside the components’ filled interiors. The joint bad-set bound makes both requirements likely.

Fix $a_1=R+2$. Reject the environment if it contains a range-$a_1$ bad component of size at least $$m_K=\left\lfloor\frac{K}{10a_1}\right\rfloor.$$ For large $K$, every remaining bad $*$-component has finite periodic lifts, by Lemma <a href="#lattice:small-lifts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lattice:small-lifts">33</a>. Reject also if either endpoint belongs to the projection of the fill of one of these components. The event on which neither rejection occurs is $\mathcal E_{v,w}$.

If the fill of a lifted $*$-component of size $m$ contains a lift of an endpoint, some root of the component lies within maximum distance $m$ of that endpoint. This follows from the bounding-box assertion and the coordinate span in Lemma <a href="#lattice:small-lifts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lattice:small-lifts">33</a>. There are at most $C m^3$ possible roots modulo $K$. Counting connected sets and using <a href="#lattice:bad-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lattice:bad-law">[lattice:bad-law]</a> thus yields <a id="lattice:rejection"></a>$$\label{lattice:rejection}
 \mathbb P(\mathcal E_{v,w}^{c})
 \le K^3(A_{a_1}p)^{m_K}
       +C\sum_{m\ge1}m^3(A_1p)^m.$$ The constant includes both endpoints. First make $p_0$ small enough that the series is at most $1/4$, then make $K_0$ large enough that the first term is at most $1/4$ for $p\le p_0$ and $K\ge K_0$. This proves $\mathbb P(\mathcal E_{v,w})\ge1/2$. We may decrease $p_0$ further below.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Nearest-neighbor detours

Draw the skeleton of Section <a href="#lattice:skeleton-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lattice:skeleton-law">8.1</a> independently of the datum. Its folding in Equation <a href="#lattice:nominal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lattice:nominal">[lattice:nominal]</a> fixes the final endpoint without conditioning the random process on a future value. We now adapt this nearest-neighbor walk to the bad set while keeping the endpoint rejection event fixed.

Figure <a href="#fig:lattice-detour" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:lattice-detour">1</a> illustrates the replacement. The construction takes place in $\mathbb Z^3$, where a finite obstacle has a well-defined exterior; projection and loop erasure are performed afterward.

<figure id="fig:lattice-detour">

<figcaption>Two parts of the path construction, shown schematically. Left: the original dashed route meets the finite bad component $C$; the solid route joins its exterior predecessor $a$ to its exterior successor $b$ through good nearest-neighbor vertices. The drawing is a planar schematic of a three-dimensional exterior shell, and does not represent holes or later loop erasure. Right: for even $n$, the random transverse displacement used in the skeleton is reflected at $n/2$; the common linear drift and integer rounding are omitted. This return to zero fixes the final endpoint without a bridge conditioning.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-lattice-detour"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:lattice-detour`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.62cm,y=0.62cm,font=\small]
 \begin{scope}
  \draw[step=1,gray!20,very thin] (0,0) grid (8,5);
  \foreach \x/\y in {3/2,3/3,4/2,4/3}
   \fill[gray!55] (\x,\y) circle (0.17);
  \draw[blue!65!black,densely dashed,line width=1.1pt]
   (0,2)--(3,2)--(3,3)--(4,3)--(4,2)--(8,2);
  \draw[green!40!black,line width=1.4pt]
   (0,2)--(2,2)--(2,1)--(5,1)--(5,2)--(8,2);
  \foreach \x/\y in {2/1,3/1,4/1,5/1,5/2,5/3,5/4,4/4,3/4,2/4,2/3,2/2}
   \draw[gray!65,fill=white] (\x,\y) circle (0.075);
  \fill (0,2) circle (0.09) node[left] {$v$};
  \fill (8,2) circle (0.09) node[right] {$w$};
  \node[above left] at (2,2) {$a$};
  \node[above right] at (5,2) {$b$};
  \node[above] at (3.5,3.1) {$C$};
  \node[align=center] at (4,-0.9) {replace the first-to-last visit};
 \end{scope}
 \begin{scope}[xshift=7cm]
  \draw[-{Latex[length=2mm]}] (0,0.4)--(6.2,0.4) node[right] {$t$};
  \draw[gray!55,dashed] (3,0.2)--(3,4.2);
  \draw[blue!65!black,line width=1.2pt]
   (0,0.4)--(0.6,1.2)--(1.2,0.9)--(1.8,2.1)--(2.4,2.5)--(3,3.3);
  \draw[green!40!black,line width=1.2pt]
   (3,3.3)--(3.6,2.5)--(4.2,2.1)--(4.8,0.9)--(5.4,1.2)--(6,0.4);
  \node[below] at (0,0.35) {$0$};
  \node[below] at (3,0.35) {$n/2$};
  \node[below] at (6,0.35) {$n$};
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 42 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Left: A schematic path routing. A horizontal dashed line segment connects an initial vertex $v$ through an exterior predecessor $a$, passes straight through a bad component region labeled $C$ enclosed by a dashed box, and reaches an exterior successor $b$, which continues to $w$. A solid green path runs from $v$ to $a$, detours around the region $C$ via multiple orthogonal segments through good nearest-neighbor vertices to reach $b$, and then continues to $w$. Below is the label "replace the first-to-last visit". Right: A coordinate plot with a horizontal axis labeled $t$, marked with ticks at $0$, $n/2$, and $n$. A curve begins at the origin $(0,0)$, rises to a peak labeled $Z_{\min(t,n-t)}^{(j)}$ at $t = n/2$, and is reflected symmetrically back down to zero at $t = n$. Below is the label "read the same displacement twice".
```


On $\mathcal E_{v,w}$, replace the visits of this lifted walk to bad components as follows. If it meets a lifted bad $*$-component $C$, take its first and last visits to $C$. The vertices immediately before and after these visits belong to $\partial_*^{\rm ext}C$: the path from the first endpoint to the first such vertex misses $C$, and the path from the last such vertex to the other endpoint misses $C$; both endpoints are outside $\operatorname{fill}(C)$. By Lemma <a href="#lattice:boundary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lattice:boundary">32</a>, join these two shell vertices by a simple nearest-neighbor path in the shell, replacing the intervening part of the walk. The added vertices are all good. Each replacement removes at least one bad visit, and no replacement introduces a bad visit. The process therefore terminates after finitely many steps.

Every component treated in this procedure was met by the original nominal refinement: any remaining bad vertex still belongs to that original walk. Project the final walk to the torus and erase loops in chronological order. The result is a simple good nearest-neighbor path from $v$ to $w$. Every nonnominal vertex belongs to the shell of a bad $*$-component met by $\Gamma$. All choices in the refinement, shell paths, and erasure can be made using fixed orderings. Thus the construction gives the measurable kernel in Proposition <a href="#prop:good-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good-paths">30</a>.

<a id="lattice:detour-charge"></a>
We now bound the encounters introduced by this construction. Use two independent nominal samples $\Gamma,\Gamma'$ in the same environment, and let $\pi,\pi'$ be their final simple paths. Let $H$ range over the range-$a_1$ bad components on the torus. Fix $C_R=52(2R+1)^3$, which also exceeds $5(2R+1)$ in Equation <a href="#lattice:pair-occurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lattice:pair-occurrence">[lattice:pair-occurrence]</a>. On $\mathcal E_{v,w}$, $$\begin{aligned}
\label{lattice:detour-charge}
 J_R(\pi,\pi')
 &\le J_R(\Gamma,\Gamma')\notag\\
 &\quad+C_R\sum_H |H|
       \mathbf 1_{\{d_\infty(H,\Gamma)\le a_1,\,
                  d_\infty(H,\Gamma')\le a_1\}}.
\end{aligned}$$ For nominal walks, $J_R$ here counts vertex occurrences, which only increases the bound.

To prove <a href="#lattice:detour-charge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lattice:detour-charge">[lattice:detour-charge]</a>, first count encounters where both vertices are nominal. These are bounded by the first term. If a nonnominal vertex belongs to the shell of $C$ and encounters a nominal vertex of the other walk, then $C$ is within distance $R+1\le a_1$ of that other walk, and $C$ meets its own nominal walk. If two nonnominal vertices belong to shells of $C,C'$, then $d_\infty(C,C')\le R+2\le a_1$, so both components belong to one range-$a_1$ component $H$ meeting both nominal walks. Charge each such encounter to one of its nonnominal vertices and to this $H$. There are at most $26|H|$ possible added vertices associated to the $*$-components inside $H$, even if several lifted copies were used: projection can only decrease this number. Each such vertex encounters at most $(2R+1)^3$ vertices of the other simple path. Charging from either path costs at most an additional factor two. This proves the claimed bound.

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
#### Averaging the environment and applying both moments

<a id="lattice:environment-expansion"></a>
Fix both complete nominal paths. Their independence from $\mathcal D$ leaves <a href="#lattice:bad-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lattice:bad-law">[lattice:bad-law]</a> available for any prescribed collection of bad vertices. Expand the exponential of the second term in <a href="#lattice:detour-charge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lattice:detour-charge">[lattice:detour-charge]</a> as a product over the actual range-$a_1$ components. A term corresponding to distinct components has disjoint vertex sets. Drop maximality, sum over all disjoint connected candidate sets, and apply <a href="#lattice:bad-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lattice:bad-law">[lattice:bad-law]</a>. Finally drop the disjointness restriction and use $1+x\le e^x$. The resulting bound is $$\begin{aligned}
\label{lattice:environment-expansion}
 &\mathbb E_{\mathcal D}\left[
 \mathbf 1_{\mathcal E_{v,w}}
 \exp\left(\kappa C_R\sum_H|H|
   \mathbf 1_{\{d_\infty(H,\Gamma),d_\infty(H,\Gamma')\le a_1\}}\right)
                       \right]\notag\\
 &\qquad\le
 \exp\left(\sum_F
       (p e^{\kappa C_R})^{|F|}
       \mathbf 1_{\{d_\infty(F,\Gamma),d_\infty(F,\Gamma')\le a_1\}}
                         \right),
\end{aligned}$$ where $F$ ranges over all nonempty range-$a_1$ connected torus sets. The factor $e^{\kappa C_R|F|}-1$ from the expansion was bounded above by $e^{\kappa C_R|F|}$. Thus only joint bad-set probabilities were used; there is no independence assumption on the environment.

We bound the exponent by the soft-distance statistic in Equation <a href="#lattice:imported-soft" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lattice:imported-soft">[lattice:imported-soft]</a>. If a contributing set $F$ has size $m$, choose an occurrence $z\in\Gamma$ within distance $a_1$ of a root of $F$. There are at most $(2a_1+1)^3$ root choices for each $z$, and at most $A_{a_1}^m$ connected sets at each root. A spanning-tree path in $F$ has at most $m-1$ edges, each of length at most $a_1$ in the periodic maximum metric. Since $F$ is also within $a_1$ of $\Gamma'$, the triangle inequality gives $$d_\infty(z,\Gamma')\le 2a_1+a_1(m-1)=a_1(m+1).$$ This argument applies even to wrapping candidate sets; only the actual components used for detours required the earlier lift and rejection test. Absorb the root choices into a constant $A_R\ge1$. The exponent in Equation <a href="#lattice:environment-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lattice:environment-expansion">[lattice:environment-expansion]</a> is at most <a id="lattice:animal-series"></a>$$\label{lattice:animal-series}
 \sum_{z\in\Gamma}\sum_{m\ge1}q^m
       \mathbf 1_{\{d_\infty(z,\Gamma')\le a_1(m+1)\}},
 \qquad q=A_Rp e^{\kappa C_R}.$$ All constants here depend only on the fixed $R$. Counting the same set more than once only increases this nonnegative upper bound.

<a id="lattice:soft-animal-sum"></a>
<a id="lattice:moment-reduction"></a>
Require $q\le e^{-2}$. If $d\le a_1(m+1)$, split $q^m$ into two equal powers and use $$q^m=q^{m/2}q^{m/2}\le q^{m/2}e^{-m}
                       \le e\,q^{m/2}e^{-d/a_1}.$$ Summing the first factor over $m\ge1$ proves $$\label{lattice:soft-animal-sum}
 \sum_{m\ge1}q^m\mathbf 1_{\{d\le a_1(m+1)\}}
 \le\eta(p,\kappa)e^{-d/a_1},
 \qquad \eta(p,\kappa)=\frac{e\sqrt q}{1-\sqrt q}.$$ In particular $\eta(p,\kappa)\to0$ as $p\to0$ at fixed $\kappa$. Set $B=a_1$. Equations <a href="#lattice:detour-charge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lattice:detour-charge">[lattice:detour-charge]</a>, <a href="#lattice:pair-occurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lattice:pair-occurrence">[lattice:pair-occurrence]</a>, and <a href="#lattice:environment-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lattice:environment-expansion">[lattice:environment-expansion]</a>–<a href="#lattice:soft-animal-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lattice:soft-animal-sum">[lattice:soft-animal-sum]</a> give $$\label{lattice:moment-reduction}
 \mathbb E_{\mathcal D}\left[
  \mathbf 1_{\mathcal E_{v,w}}e^{\kappa J_R(\pi,\pi')}
       \mid\Gamma,\Gamma'\right]
 \le\exp\left(\kappa C_RJ_{\rm occ}^{(R)}+
                      \eta(p,\kappa)S_B(\Gamma,\Gamma')\right).$$ On the left, the adapted paths are used only on $\mathcal E_{v,w}$. The environment was averaged once, with both entire skeletons fixed.

First choose $\kappa>0$ with $2\kappa C_R\le\alpha_R$, where $\alpha_R=c_{\rm hit}/(1+R)^2$. Next choose $p_0>0$ so small that $q\le e^{-2}$ and $2\eta(p,\kappa)\le\xi(B)$ for $p\le p_0$, as well as the earlier endpoint rejection requirements. The constants $K_0$ can then be enlarged to cover those rejection estimates. Cauchy–Schwarz and both moments in Lemma <a href="#lem:skeleton" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:skeleton">31</a> imply $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\exp\left(\kappa C_RJ_{\rm occ}^{(R)}+\eta S_B\right)
 &\le
 \left(\mathbb Ee^{2\kappa C_RJ_{\rm occ}^{(R)}}\right)^{1/2}
 \left(\mathbb Ee^{2\eta S_B}\right)^{1/2}\\
 &\le\sqrt{C_{\rm hit}C(B)}=:C_{\rm path}<\infty.
\end{aligned}$$ Together with Equation <a href="#lattice:moment-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lattice:moment-reduction">[lattice:moment-reduction]</a>, this proves the annealed bound <a href="#eq:lattice-encounter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-encounter">[eq:lattice-encounter]</a>. Both path draws use one common datum and independent skeleton seeds. The event retains its original mass; there is no renormalization by conditioning on acceptance and no assertion of a bound for each fixed environment.

Finally, for $K\ge80$ the interval $[\lceil K/10\rceil,\lfloor K/8\rfloor]$ contains at least $K/40-1\ge K/80$ integers. For each such $n$, there are $(2n+1)^2\ge K^2/25$ transverse choices. They are distinct modulo $K$, since $2n<K$. Multiplying by the $K^3$ choices of starting vertex gives at least $K^6/2000=\upsilon K^6$ admissible ordered pairs. This completes Proposition <a href="#prop:good-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good-paths">30</a>.

<a id="sec:comparison"></a>
### A common measure and positive occupation

We now turn the supply of movable labels into a lower bound on the constant-orbital fraction $B_{\rm occ}$. Recall from Proposition <a href="#prop:purification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:purification">14</a> that the midpoint configuration $X$ and the retained configuration $W'$ have density $\Phi(X,W')^2$, where $\Phi$ is the nonnegative kernel of the square root of the canonical state. The input is the conditional path law of Section <a href="#sec:groups" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:groups">7</a> and the good lattice routes of Proposition <a href="#prop:good-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good-paths">30</a>. Along one route we construct two copies of the selected trajectories. Their midpoint assignments leave the same observed bath after different tags are removed. We then bound the second moment of the resulting change of measure by encounters between two routes.

This is the comparison mechanism of OpenAI (2026b, sec. 8) <a id="pm-cite-66"></a>[[HS](#ref-HS)]; its conditional normalizer argument is reproduced below. The thermal modification is to retain $W'$ in every observation and in the exposed data. It is never resampled. All constants obtained here are uniform in $D,K$ and $0<T\le D^{-1}$ once the earlier fixed parameters have been chosen.

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### Affinity and a common finite measure

<a id="cmp:affinity-superadditive"></a>
For finite nonnegative measures $P,Q$ on the same measurable space, define their affinity by $$\mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}(P,Q)=\int\sqrt{pq}\,\mathrm d\xi,
 \qquad p=\frac{\,\mathrm dP}{\,\mathrm d\xi},\quad q=\frac{\,\mathrm dQ}{\,\mathrm d\xi},
 \quad \xi=P+Q.$$ Using another dominating measure gives the same value. We will use three elementary properties. If $P\ge P'$ and $Q\ge Q'$, then $\mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}(P,Q)\ge\mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}(P',Q')$. For finite lists of measure pairs, $$\label{cmp:affinity-superadditive}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}\left(\sum_jP_j,\sum_jQ_j\right)
 \ge\sum_j\mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}(P_j,Q_j).$$ Indeed, pointwise Cauchy–Schwarz gives $\sum_j\sqrt{p_jq_j}\le\sqrt{(\sum_jp_j)(\sum_jq_j)}$. Finally, applying the same probability kernel to both measures cannot decrease affinity. To see this, form the joint measures with that kernel and then condition a common dominating measure on the output. Conditional Cauchy–Schwarz gives $\mathbb E(\sqrt{pq}\mid\text{output})\le
\sqrt{\mathbb E(p\mid\text{output})\mathbb E(q\mid\text{output})}$. Integration proves the assertion. Projection is a special case.

<a id="stmt-lem-affinity"></a>
<div id="lem:affinity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 34** (A common finite measure). *Let $P,Q$ be finite nonnegative measures and let $\nu$ be a finite measure of mass $m>0$, absolutely continuous with respect to both. If $$\int\left(\frac{\,\mathrm d\nu}{\,\mathrm dP}\right)^2\,\mathrm dP\le C_P,
 \qquad
 \int\left(\frac{\,\mathrm d\nu}{\,\mathrm dQ}\right)^2\,\mathrm dQ\le C_Q,$$ then $\mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}(P,Q)\ge m^2/\sqrt{C_PC_Q}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-affinity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-affinity</summary>

*Proof.* With densities $p,q,s$ relative to a common dominating measure, Hölder’s inequality with exponents $4,4,2$ gives $$m=\int
 \left(\frac{s^2}{p}\right)^{1/4}
 \left(\frac{s^2}{q}\right)^{1/4}(pq)^{1/4}
 \le C_P^{1/4}C_Q^{1/4}\mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}(P,Q)^{1/2}.$$ The ratios can be set to zero where their denominators vanish, since $s$ is zero there. Squaring and rearranging proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### Cell measures and disjoint groups of tags

<a id="cmp:cell-affinity"></a>
Recall $V=K^3$, $D=N/V$, and the unit cells $B_v$, each of volume one. Write $X'=(x_2,\ldots,x_N)$ for the ordered bath. For ordered cells $v,w$, define finite measures on $(x,y,X',W')\in B_v\times B_w\times\Lambda_K^{N-1}\times\Lambda_K^N$ by <a id="cmp:cell-measures"></a>$$\label{cmp:cell-measures}
\begin{aligned}
 \,\mathrm dP_{vw}=V\Phi(x,X',W')^2\,\mathrm dx\,\mathrm dy\,\mathrm dX'\,\mathrm dW',
 \\
 \,\mathrm dQ_{vw}=V\Phi(y,X',W')^2\,\mathrm dx\,\mathrm dy\,\mathrm dX'\,\mathrm dW'.
\end{aligned}$$ Nonnegativity of $\Phi$ and the occupation formula in Proposition <a href="#prop:purification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:purification">14</a> give the exact identity $$\label{cmp:cell-affinity}
 B_{\rm occ}=\frac1{V^2}\sum_{v,w}\mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}(P_{vw},Q_{vw}).$$

Fix a group index $1\le g\le G_D:=\lfloor\ell D/(4M)\rfloor$ from Section <a href="#sec:groups" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:groups">7</a>. Its exposed data are $\mathcal B$, its unexposed label set is $\mathcal I$, and its conditional path law is $\lambda_{\mathcal I}$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-law">27</a>. Define measures $\widehat P_g,\widehat Q_g$ on $(\mathcal B,x,y,X')$; the data $\mathcal B$ already include $W'$. For $\widehat P_g$, sample the data and the conditional unexposed paths, and *sum* the following contributions over all $i\in\mathcal I$: take $x=\omega_i(t)$, restrict to $x\in B_v$, order the remaining $N-1$ midpoints by a fresh uniform permutation to form $X'$, and sample $y$ uniformly in $B_w$. For $\widehat Q_g$, take the tagged midpoint as $y\in B_w$ and sample the extra point $x$ uniformly in $B_v$. The use of a sum, rather than a uniformly chosen tag, fixes their normalization.

<a id="stmt-lem-tag-normalization"></a>
<div id="lem:tag-normalization" class="lemma">

<a id="cmp:group-affinity"></a>
**Lemma 35** (Tag normalization). *For each ordered pair of cells, $$\label{cmp:group-affinity}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}(P_{vw},Q_{vw})
 \ge\frac1D\sum_{g=1}^{G_D}\mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}(\widehat P_g,\widehat Q_g).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-tag-normalization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tag-normalization</summary>

*Proof.* Put all groups on the original slab sample, with all allocation randomizations and a fresh uniform permutation of the full label set. After deleting a tag, the restricted permutation uniformly orders its bath. Each group’s conditional construction is a disintegration of this common sample with respect to its own exposed data. Thus the differing data spaces are projected away before the following comparison; no conditional symmetry of the unexposed-path law is asserted. For every sampled path configuration, group label sets are disjoint. Their sum of tag contributions is bounded by the sum over all $N$ trajectory labels. The unrestricted midpoint vector together with $W'$ has density $\Phi(X,W')^2$, symmetric in $X$ for each $W'$. For each fixed tag label, uniform ordering of the bath therefore gives the same density $\Phi(x,X',W')^2$. Summing all tags gives $N$ times that density. Since the extra target cell has volume one and $V/N=1/D$, projecting to $(x,y,X',W')$ yields $$\frac1D\sum_g\operatorname{proj}\widehat P_g\le P_{vw},
 \qquad
 \frac1D\sum_g\operatorname{proj}\widehat Q_g\le Q_{vw}.$$ Use monotonicity, <a href="#cmp:affinity-superadditive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:affinity-superadditive">[cmp:affinity-superadditive]</a>, and the increase of affinity under projection. Its homogeneity supplies the factor $1/D$. ◻

</details>

The remaining task is to find, for each retained group and each of many ordered cell pairs, a common measure of mass at least $1/2$ whose squared likelihood integrals against $\widehat P_g$ and $\widehat Q_g$ are bounded by one constant $C_2$. Lemma <a href="#lem:affinity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:affinity">34</a> would then give affinity at least $1/(4C_2)$ per group. The factor $1/D$ in Equation <a href="#cmp:group-affinity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:group-affinity">[cmp:group-affinity]</a> can be paid because the number of disjoint groups is proportional to $D$.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### Two midpoint assignments with the same observed bath

Take $R=30$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:good-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good-paths">30</a>, and fix an admissible ordered cell pair $v,w$: its lift displacement $(n,d_2,d_3)$ satisfies $\lceil K/10\rceil\le n\le\lfloor K/8\rfloor$ and $|d_i|\le n$. Choose the good-site parameters of Proposition <a href="#prop:group-supply" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:group-supply">28</a> to meet the bad-set hypothesis of Proposition <a href="#prop:good-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good-paths">30</a>. Let $\mathcal E_{vw}$ be the data-measurable event supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:good-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good-paths">30</a>, of probability at least $1/2$. On this event draw a simple nearest-neighbor path $$z_0=v,z_1,\ldots,z_d=w$$ through good sites. For each $k<d$, independently choose a uniform accessible label $i_k$ anchored at $z_k$. The choices inspect only $\mathcal B$ and the lattice path. In particular, they do not inspect any latent trajectory. The selected labels are distinct because their anchors are distinct.

We construct two path configurations that share their observed $(x,y,X',W')$. Keep the exposed data fixed. First sample all unselected unexposed trajectories from their exact marginal under $\lambda_{\mathcal I}$, and use them in both copies. For every vertex $z_k$, prepare a factor as follows. The first copy needs label $i_k$ through this cell if $k<d$, and the second copy needs label $i_{k-1}$ through it if $k>0$. Intersect the corresponding good-target sets $T_{i_k}(z_k)$ and $T_{i_{k-1}}(z_k)$ from Section <a href="#sec:groups" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:groups">7</a>, using only the one required set at an endpoint. The intersection, denoted by $A_k$, has volume at least $0.98$. Sample $U_k$ uniformly in $A_k$, and then independently sample the one or two required bridge trials through $U_k$, each conditioned on its individual success against the frozen paths. All $d+1$ vertex factors are independent before the additional conditioning below. Figure <a href="#cmp:shift-figure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cmp:shift-figure">2</a> shows the two midpoint assignments.

Use $U_d$ as the extra point in the first copy and $U_0$ as the extra point in the second copy. Impose all remaining hard-avoidance checks in both copies: selected paths must avoid the unselected unexposed paths and one another within their respective copies. Condition the product of vertex factors on these checks, separately for each fixed collection of unselected paths. The sampled unselected-path marginal therefore remains its original marginal under $\lambda_{\mathcal I}$. More explicitly, let $\lambda_{\rm out}$ be that marginal, let $F_k$ be the probability law of the $k$th vertex factor, and let $J$ be the indicator of the remaining checks in both copies. The joint law just defined is <a id="cmp:fixed-outside-law"></a>$$\label{cmp:fixed-outside-law}
 \lambda_{\rm out}(\,\mathrm d\omega_{\rm out})\,
 \frac{J(\omega_{\rm out},\xi_0,\ldots,\xi_d)
             \prod_{k=0}^d F_k(\,\mathrm d\xi_k)}
      {\displaystyle\int J(\omega_{\rm out},\xi_0,\ldots,\xi_d)
             \prod_{k=0}^d F_k(\,\mathrm d\xi_k)}.$$ The denominator is a function of the fixed outside paths. Integrating the second factor gives one for each such configuration. A single normalization after integrating the outside paths would change their marginal and would not yield the comparison used below. Each $F_k$ depends only on the data, route and selected labels, so it is fixed before those outside paths are sampled.

<a id="cmp:compatibility"></a>
We verify that this conditional construction is defined. A vertex factor carries at most two paths, each with a marginal of the form $\Pi_i^{A_k}$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:group-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:group-comparison">29</a>. Its paths are confined within distance ten of their anchors, and there are at most $M$ unexposed labels per anchor. Consequently each factor has at most $C_{\rm col}M$ possible opponents in the two copies, for a fixed geometric constant $C_{\rm col}$. All opponents have distinct labels within the copy in which a check is imposed. The deterministic-opponent estimate, summed over those checks, gives $$\label{cmp:compatibility}
 \chi_D=C_{\rm col}M e_D\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(D\longrightarrow\infty,\ M\text{ fixed}).$$ For any fixed regular and confined values of the other paths with their recorded endpoints, the factor passes every incident check with probability at least $1-\chi_D$. This statement averages its common midpoint over $A_k$; it makes no claim at a prescribed midpoint. It uses the two path marginals and a union bound, so no independence of the paths within the factor is required. Inserting the $d+1$ factors successively now bounds the two-copy conditioning probability below by $(1-\chi_D)^{d+1}>0$ for sufficiently large $D$.

<a id="cmp:common-mass"></a>
Tag $i_0$ in the first copy and $i_{d-1}$ in the second. In each copy the tag and the extra point then give $x=U_0$, $y=U_d$. The selected bath midpoints in both copies are exactly $U_1,\ldots,U_{d-1}$; the unselected midpoints also agree. Choose a common uniform ordering of the shared midpoint instances; their physical labels in the two copies may differ. In each copy separately, this has the uniform ordering law determined by its paths and its tag. This defines a single finite measure $\widehat\nu$ on $(\mathcal B,x,y,X')$ after averaging the data, routes and labels, with zero mass off $\mathcal E_{vw}$. Each conditional sampling law on this event is a probability measure, so $$\label{cmp:common-mass}
 \widehat\nu(\text{all})=\mathbb P(\mathcal E_{vw})\ge\frac12.$$ These are measurable probability kernels: the route and label choices are finite, the target sets are measurable functions of the data, and all path restrictions are Borel subsets of the continuous-path spaces. The positive normalizers just proved permit the displayed conditioning.

<figure id="cmp:shift-figure">

<figcaption>The midpoint assignment for a route of three edges. A dashed column is one vertex factor with one common midpoint. Arrows identify the same particle label in the two copies; they are not trajectories in time. Removing the highlighted tags leaves the same two selected bath midpoints. All unselected bath paths and the configuration $W′$ are shared. For a route of arbitrary length the interior columns continue in the same way.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
#### Cancellation of conditional normalizers

The midpoint construction has two path copies in each internal vertex factor. To estimate its likelihood, designate one copy as primary and regard the other as auxiliary. At an internal vertex $z_k$, $1\le k<d$, the primary path has label $i_k$ in the first-copy view, whereas the auxiliary path has label $i_{k-1}$. Thus deleting a factor is not the same operation as deleting all instances of one particle label. The following lemma keeps this distinction explicit and isolates the normalizers that cancel when two routes are far apart. Its coordinates and factors are abstract; we identify them with the midpoint construction immediately after the proof. The two changes arise because the square of a route-averaged likelihood expands into a product for two independent route choices at the same exposed datum.

The next lemma, from OpenAI (2026b, Lemma 8.3) <a id="pm-cite-67"></a>[[HS](#ref-HS)], concerns finitely many coordinates in standard Borel spaces, each with a probability reference $P_i$. All auxiliary spaces below are standard Borel as well. A *check* will mean an indicator imposing a constraint on one or two coordinates. The joint reference law $\lambda$ is the product $\bigotimes_iP_i$ conditioned on a finite collection of checks. An additional independent coordinate is allowed; it simply has no checks.

<a id="cmp:factor-density"></a>
A change indexed by a set $S$ leaves the marginal outside $S$ unchanged. Inside $S$, it starts from independent factors $\Pi_{S,i}$, $i\in S$. Each factor includes a *primary coordinate* in the space of $P_i$ and possibly auxiliary coordinates. Its primary marginal is $g_{S,i}P_i$, with $$\label{cmp:factor-density}
 0\le g_{S,i}\le G,
 \qquad \int g_{S,i}\,\mathrm dP_i=1.$$ The factors are fixed before the outside coordinates are sampled. The product is conditioned on specified checks, including every reference check involving the primary coordinates in $S$. Checks involving only fixed outside coordinates are already satisfied almost surely under the unchanged outside marginal; otherwise the conditional proposal would not be defined there. Omit these redundant checks. Every remaining proposal check therefore involves a coordinate carried by a changed factor. Auxiliary coordinates are then forgotten. The resulting primary likelihood relative to $\lambda$ is denoted by $L_S$.

For each primary and auxiliary coordinate, fix a measurable allowed set on which all its relevant reference and proposal marginals are concentrated. These sets, like the proposal factors, are fixed before sampling outside coordinates. A configuration is *individually permitted* when each coordinate lies in its allowed set; this imposes no pairwise checks. The compatibility hypothesis below is uniform on these sets, including configurations that fail some pairwise checks.

We make explicit the deletion convention for this construction. When some factors are removed, remove their coordinates and all checks incident to them. Do not replace a removed auxiliary coordinate by an original reference coordinate. Coordinates fixed outside the sets under discussion remain as obstacles in all the checks in which they occur. Coordinates here are path instances in a specified copy. If an auxiliary with label $j$ belongs to a retained factor, it remains integrated in that factor even when a different factor carrying the primary label $j$ is deleted. It is never replaced by the original path with label $j$.

<a id="stmt-lem-cancellation"></a>
<div id="lem:cancellation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 36** (Cancellation away from an overlap). *Consider two changes $S,T$ of the preceding kind, with $G\ge1$, and put $U=S\cup T$. Suppose that the following conditions hold.*

1.  *There is $\chi\in[0,1)$ with two insertion bounds. In the reference system, inserting any coordinate $i\in U$ from $P_i$ satisfies all reference checks incident to $i$ with probability at least $1-\chi$. In the proposal system of either change, inserting a whole factor from $\Pi_{S,i}$ or $\Pi_{T,i}$ satisfies all checks of that proposal incident to its carried coordinates with probability at least $1-\chi$. Each bound is uniform in every fixed choice of the other individually permitted coordinates of the respective system, and remains valid after deleting checks or coordinates as specified above.*

2.  *There are decompositions $S=C_S\sqcup F_S$ and $T=C_T\sqcup F_T$, with $F_S\cap T=F_T\cap S=\varnothing$. No coordinate carried by an $F_S$ factor has a check with any coordinate carried by a $T$ factor, or with the original primary coordinates indexed by $T$; the corresponding assertion holds with $S,T$ interchanged. The reference coordinates in $F_S,F_T$ also have no mutual checks.*

<a id="cmp:cancellation-bound"></a>
*Then $$\label{cmp:cancellation-bound}
 \int L_SL_T\,\mathrm d\lambda
 \le G^{|S\cap T|}(1-\chi)^{-2(|C_S|+|C_T|)}.$$ It is enough that the assumptions hold for almost every fixed admissible configuration outside $U$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cancellation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cancellation</summary>

*Proof.* Put $a_\chi=1-\chi$ and $c=|C_S|+|C_T|$. Fix the primary coordinates outside $U$. All normalizers below are conditional on these fixed values; we first work on a configuration for which this conditional law is defined.

For any selected set, its reference normalizer is the integral of its reference-check indicator against its product references. In particular, write $Z_U$ for this integral over $U$. When the coordinates outside $S$ are fixed, write $Z_S$ for the corresponding integral over $S$. Thus $Z_S$ can depend on coordinates in $T\setminus S$. Write $Y_S$ for the integral of the proposal-check indicator $J_S$ against $\bigotimes_{i\in S}\Pi_{S,i}$, with the same outside values fixed.

<a id="cmp:exact-likelihood"></a>
Disintegrate each factor in the form $$\Pi_{S,i}(\,\mathrm dx_i\,\,\mathrm d\zeta_i)
   =P_i(\,\mathrm dx_i)K_{S,i}(x_i,\,\mathrm d\zeta_i),
 \qquad K_{S,i}(x_i,\text{all})=g_{S,i}(x_i).$$ Here $\zeta_i$ denotes all its auxiliary coordinates. Define $$H_S(x_S;x_{S^c})
  =\int J_S\prod_{i\in S}K_{S,i}(x_i,\,\mathrm d\zeta_i).$$ On the reference-admissible configurations, the exact likelihood is $$\label{cmp:exact-likelihood}
 L_S=\frac{Z_S}{Y_S}H_S.$$ Indeed, the conditional reference density is its check indicator divided by $Z_S$, whereas the new primary density is $H_S/Y_S$, both relative to $\bigotimes_{i\in S}P_i$. The outside marginal is identical. The inclusion of primary reference checks ensures that the new density is zero on reference-forbidden configurations.

Now retain only the factors indexed by $F_S$, together with coordinates fixed outside $U$, using the stated deletion convention. Denote their reference normalizer, proposal normalizer and primary proposal density by $Z_{F_S},Y_{F_S},H_{F_S}$. Define the three corresponding $F_T$ quantities using the factors from the $T$ change. These quantities depend only on the outside of $U$ and, for $H_{F_S},H_{F_T}$, on their own primary coordinates. They do not depend on the omitted near coordinates.

<a id="cmp:union-normalizer"></a>
Successive insertion using assumption <span class="upright">(i)</span>, and noninteraction in assumption <span class="upright">(ii)</span>, give <a id="cmp:single-normalizers"></a><a id="cmp:other-normalizers"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 Z_U&\ge a_\chi^c Z_{F_S}Z_{F_T},
       \label{cmp:union-normalizer}\\
 Z_S&\le Z_{F_S},\qquad
 Y_S\ge a_\chi^{|C_S|}Y_{F_S},
       \label{cmp:single-normalizers}\\
 Z_T&\le Z_{F_T},\qquad
 Y_T\ge a_\chi^{|C_T|}Y_{F_T}.
       \label{cmp:other-normalizers}
\end{aligned}$$ For the first inequality, the reference integral over the two far sets factorizes. The remaining distinct coordinates form $U\setminus(F_S\cup F_T)$, a subset of $C_S\cup C_T$ with cardinality at most $c$. Insert them one at a time with the uniform compatibility bound. For the upper bound on $Z_S$, delete the checks involving $C_S$ and integrate those reference coordinates freely. Coordinates in $T\setminus S$ have no checks with the far set. For its proposal lower bound, start with the reduced $F_S$ integral and add the $C_S$ factors. The fixed original coordinates in $T\setminus S$ still do not meet the far factors. An auxiliary instance carried by a retained factor remains integrated there, even if a newly inserted factor carries its primary label; only the checks of the $S$ proposal are restored. Each addition retains at least the factor $a_\chi$. Applying these two arguments to the $T$ change proves the last line. These statements are pointwise in the opposing coordinates; they do not assert that $Z_S$ or $Y_S$ is constant in them.

The insertion bounds also prove positivity of the divisors. In particular, integrating coordinates one at a time gives a positive product lower bound for every reduced normalizer. The full reference conditional is defined on the outside configurations under consideration.

<a id="cmp:kernel-product"></a>
Deleting proposal checks involving a near factor gives $$H_S\le H_{F_S}\prod_{i\in C_S}g_{S,i},
 \qquad
 H_T\le H_{F_T}\prod_{i\in C_T}g_{T,i}.$$ After dropping the reference admissibility indicator in an upper bound, the two far primary integrals equal $Y_{F_S}$ and $Y_{F_T}$. The remaining coordinates carry only the displayed marginal densities. A coordinate occurring once integrates to one. A coordinate occurring in both lists costs at most $G$, by <a href="#cmp:factor-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:factor-density">[cmp:factor-density]</a>. Therefore $$\label{cmp:kernel-product}
 \int H_SH_T\mathbf 1_{\mathrm{admissible}}
              \prod_{i\in U}P_i(\,\mathrm dx_i)
 \le Y_{F_S}Y_{F_T}G^{|S\cap T|}.$$ The conditional reference law on $U$ has normalizer $Z_U$. Combine <a href="#cmp:exact-likelihood" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:exact-likelihood">[cmp:exact-likelihood]</a>–<a href="#cmp:kernel-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:kernel-product">[cmp:kernel-product]</a>. The two pointwise ratio bounds contribute $a_\chi^{-c}Z_{F_S}Z_{F_T}/(Y_{F_S}Y_{F_T})$; division by $Z_U$ contributes at most $a_\chi^{-c}/(Z_{F_S}Z_{F_T})$. All four far normalizers cancel, leaving $G^{|S\cap T|}a_\chi^{-2c}$. Finally integrate the outside coordinates. This proves <a href="#cmp:cancellation-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:cancellation-bound">[cmp:cancellation-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### The likelihood cost is confined to encounters

Fix the data, the route and the selected labels. Regard either copy as the primary one. The reference for that copy is $\lambda_{\mathcal I}\otimes P_*$, where $P_*$ is the uniform law of the extra point in its endpoint cell. Let $$S=\{i_0,\ldots,i_{d-1}\}\cup\{*\}.$$ In Lemma <a href="#lem:cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cancellation">36</a>, a primary coordinate is the chosen copy’s path or endpoint extra point. Its auxiliary coordinates are the other copy’s path or extra point through the same midpoint. The checks are the collision exclusions within each copy; there is no collision check between paths in different copies. Index a vertex factor by its primary label, using $*$ for the extra point; its center is its midpoint cell $z_k$. The two paths in one factor may have different labels, and each copy contributes at most one path. The allowed set for each path consists of the regular, confined, frozen-avoiding paths with its own recorded endpoints. Each extra point lies in its target cell. These are individual restrictions; the remaining collision checks still couple different factors.

By Proposition <a href="#prop:group-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:group-comparison">29</a>, the primary marginal of each path factor has density at most $G$ relative to $P_i$, and integrates to one. For the extra point the density is $\mathbf 1_{A_k}/|A_k|$ relative to $P_*$, which is at most $1/0.98$. Enlarge the fixed $G$ to be at least two, covering this factor as well. These marginal densities and their auxiliary kernels depend on the exposed data and the route and label choices; they do not depend on the unselected unexposed paths.

The union bound proving Equation <a href="#cmp:compatibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:compatibility">[cmp:compatibility]</a> verifies hypothesis <span class="upright">(i)</span> for each proposal factor. The same argument with $P_i$ in place of $\Pi_i^{A_k}$ verifies it for insertion of a selected reference path, by Proposition <a href="#prop:group-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:group-comparison">29</a>. The reference extra point has no checks. These estimates are uniform over all individually permitted opponent paths, whether or not their labels are accessible, and deleting checks can only improve them. Thus the bound remains available for every reduced normalizer in Lemma <a href="#lem:cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cancellation">36</a>.

Take two independent route and label choices at the same data and endpoint pair, and denote their selected index sets by $S,T$. For the chosen primary side, declare a factor of $S$ near if its center is at lattice supremum distance at most $R$ from some center of $T$, and declare every other factor far. Define $C_S,F_S,C_T,F_T$ accordingly. A shared real label has centers at distance at most two in the two assignments, so it is near; the shared extra index has the same endpoint center. Consequently the far index sets are disjoint from the opposing lists.

All paths carried by a factor lie within distance eleven of its center. The original path of an opposing real label also lies within distance eleven of that opposing factor’s center. Since $R=30$, far factors cannot interact with any coordinate carried by the opposite list or with those original paths. This includes the auxiliary-copy paths. Far sets therefore meet the separation assumptions of Lemma <a href="#lem:cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cancellation">36</a>.

For clarity, a reduced far proposal retains exactly the paths carried by its far vertex factors and the fixed paths outside $S\cup T$. It deletes the omitted near factors in both copies. It does not put their original trajectories back as unselected obstacles. With this convention all the normalizers in <a href="#cmp:union-normalizer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:union-normalizer">[cmp:union-normalizer]</a>–<a href="#cmp:kernel-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:kernel-product">[cmp:kernel-product]</a> are the ones arising from the present construction.

<a id="cmp:two-choice-bound"></a>
Write $L_S,L_T$ for the two primary likelihoods. If $$J_R=\#\{(j,k):d_\infty(z_j,z'_k)\le R\},$$ then $|C_S|+|C_T|\le2J_R$. Lemma <a href="#lem:cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cancellation">36</a> gives, at the fixed data, $$\label{cmp:two-choice-bound}
 \int L_SL_T\,\mathrm d(\lambda_{\mathcal I}\otimes P_*)
 \le G^{|S\cap T|}(1-\chi_D)^{-4J_R}.$$ In particular, no factor is paid for a long portion of one route that stays away from the other.

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
#### Averaging labels and forgetting the paths

<a id="cmp:M-choice"></a>
<a id="cmp:D-choice"></a>
Condition on the data and the two lattice paths before choosing their labels. Each common departure cell has at least $M/2$ accessible labels. The two uniform choices at that cell match with probability at most $2/M$, and the match events are independent between distinct common cells. Real labels anchored at different cells cannot agree. The shared extra point contributes one factor $G$, so <a id="cmp:label-average"></a>$$\label{cmp:label-average}
 \mathbb E_{\mathrm{labels}}G^{|S\cap T|}
 \le G\exp\left(\frac{2(G-1)}M J_R\right).$$ Choose the fixed integer $M$ sufficiently large for the good-site supply and for $$\label{cmp:M-choice}
 \frac{2(G-1)}M\le\frac\kappa2.$$ Then increase $D_*$ so that, for every $D\ge D_*$ in the allowed $\alpha$ window, all earlier estimates hold and $$\label{cmp:D-choice}
 -4\log(1-\chi_D)\le\frac\kappa2,
 \qquad
 \frac{G_D}{D}\ge\frac\ell{8M}.$$ The first requirement is possible by <a href="#cmp:compatibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:compatibility">[cmp:compatibility]</a>; the second follows once $\ell D/(4M)\ge2$. These choices do not change $G$, $\kappa$, or $C_{\mathrm{path}}$.

The following use of an averaged likelihood and a two-route second moment also occurs in the point-reassignment argument of Peres and Sly (Peres and Sly 2014, Proposition 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-68"></a>[[PeresSly2014](#ref-PeresSly2014)]. Here Lemma <a href="#lem:cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cancellation">36</a> supplies the additional conditional-normalizer estimate required by the hard-core path law.

Let $\overline L$ be the likelihood averaged over the conditional route and label choices, with value zero off $\mathcal E_{vw}$. Tonelli’s theorem expresses its square integral using two independent such choices. Combining <a href="#cmp:two-choice-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:two-choice-bound">[cmp:two-choice-bound]</a>–<a href="#cmp:D-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:D-choice">[cmp:D-choice]</a> and the annealed encounter bound in Proposition <a href="#prop:good-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good-paths">30</a> gives <a id="cmp:latent-second-moment"></a>$$\label{cmp:latent-second-moment}
 \mathbb E_{\mathcal B}\int\overline L^{2}
           \,\mathrm d(\lambda_{\mathcal I}\otimes P_*)
 \le G\mathbb E\bigl[\mathbf 1_{\mathcal E_{vw}}e^{\kappa J_R}\bigr]
 \le C_2,
 \qquad C_2:=G C_{\mathrm{path}}.$$ Here the two route randomizations share the same data; the expectation over the data is taken only once. The event is not conditioned to have probability one. Its indicator is part of both likelihoods, and its square is itself. The calculation applies separately with either copy primary, with the same constant $C_2$.

We next check carefully that tagging and observing the bath do not increase this bound. At fixed data, split $\overline L=\sum_{i\in\mathcal I}f_i$, where $f_i$ contains exactly the choices designating $i$ as the primary tag. The functions are nonnegative. In the first copy $f_i$ is supported where $\omega_i(t)\in B_v$; in the second it is supported where $\omega_i(t)\in B_w$. Since $$\sum_i f_i^2\le\left(\sum_i f_i\right)^2,$$ the measure that retains the tag index has squared likelihood integral at most $C_2$ relative to the *sum* of the tag-restricted reference measures on the disjoint copies indexed by $i$. Now apply the observation kernel that orders the bath and retains $\mathcal B,x,y,X'$, and forget the tag and all the paths. If a finite measure has density $f$ relative to a reference $P$, the density after applying a common kernel is the conditional average of $f$ in the corresponding joint reference measure. Conditional Cauchy–Schwarz bounds its squared integral by $\int f^2\,\mathrm dP$. This remains valid for finite reference measures, by normalization when their mass is nonzero.

<a id="cmp:one-group-gain"></a>
For either primary copy, the resulting measure is the common observed $\widehat\nu$ already constructed. Its corresponding reference is precisely $\widehat P_g$ or $\widehat Q_g$. We have proved <a id="cmp:observed-second-moments"></a>$$\label{cmp:observed-second-moments}
 \int\left(\frac{\,\mathrm d\widehat\nu}{\,\mathrm d\widehat P_g}\right)^2
                \,\mathrm d\widehat P_g\le C_2,
 \qquad
 \int\left(\frac{\,\mathrm d\widehat\nu}{\,\mathrm d\widehat Q_g}\right)^2
                \,\mathrm d\widehat Q_g\le C_2.$$ Together with <a href="#cmp:common-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:common-mass">[cmp:common-mass]</a>, Lemma <a href="#lem:affinity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:affinity">34</a> now gives $$\label{cmp:one-group-gain}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}(\widehat P_g,\widehat Q_g)\ge\frac1{4C_2}.$$ This lower bound is uniform in the group index and in every admissible ordered pair of cells.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cancellation-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cancellation</summary>

<a id="cmp:c-star"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:scaled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:scaled">2</a>.* With the fixed choices above, set $$\label{cmp:c-star}
 c_*:=\frac{\upsilon\ell}{32M G C_{\mathrm{path}}}>0,
 \qquad \upsilon=\frac1{2000}.$$ For large $K$, at least $\upsilon V^2$ ordered cell pairs meet the displacement condition of Proposition <a href="#prop:good-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good-paths">30</a>. For each such pair, Lemma <a href="#lem:tag-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tag-normalization">35</a>, <a href="#cmp:D-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:D-choice">[cmp:D-choice]</a> and <a href="#cmp:one-group-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:one-group-gain">[cmp:one-group-gain]</a> give $$\mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}(P_{vw},Q_{vw})
 \ge\frac{G_D}{4D C_2}
 \ge\frac\ell{32M C_2}.$$ Sum these nonnegative contributions in <a href="#cmp:cell-affinity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:cell-affinity">[cmp:cell-affinity]</a> to obtain $B_{\rm occ}\ge\upsilon\ell/(32M C_2)$, which is <a href="#cmp:c-star" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:c-star">[cmp:c-star]</a>. Every estimate used above is uniform over the stated temperature interval. The volume threshold is locally uniform for $D$ in compact subintervals of $[D_*,\infty)$, by the slab, bridge and group estimates and Proposition <a href="#prop:good-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good-paths">30</a>. No parameter entering $c_*$ is chosen after $D_*$, so increasing $D_*$ does not change the occupation bound.

For completeness, the choices leading to this bound have no circular dependence. Fix the lattice radius $R=30$ and its bad-site tolerance from Proposition <a href="#prop:good-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good-paths">30</a>. Make the spatial choices of large $\alpha_0$, then $\ell,\theta$ and small $b$ as in Sections <a href="#sec:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:energy">3</a> and <a href="#sec:spatial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:spatial">4</a>. Choose a sufficiently small target certificate-failure base $p_1$. Proposition <a href="#prop:certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:certificates">17</a> then allows $H_1$ to be fixed before choosing a sufficiently small positive $t$, also meeting the deterministic bridge requirements. This fixes the density bound $G$. Choose $M$ large enough for the group supply and <a href="#cmp:M-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:M-choice">[cmp:M-choice]</a>; finally choose $D_*$ large enough for all small-scale requirements and <a href="#cmp:D-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:D-choice">[cmp:D-choice]</a>. The required lower bound on $K$ is imposed afterwards. In particular $\beta\ge D$ is the only temperature restriction; no condition relates $\beta$ to the volume. ◻

</details>

The remaining step is the return to fixed physical density and temperature.

<a id="sec:physical"></a>
### Return to fixed physical parameters

We now prove Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> from the scaled estimate. This step also makes explicit why the temperature remains positive throughout the thermodynamic limit.

Let $\alpha_0,D_*,c_*$ be furnished by Theorem <a href="#thm:scaled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:scaled">2</a>. For a fixed exclusion distance $a>0$, set $$\rho_*(a)=\frac{\alpha_0^3}{4D_*^2a^3}.$$ Fix $0<\rho<\rho_*(a)$ and define <a id="eq:physical-temperature"></a>$$\label{eq:physical-temperature}
 s_\infty=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_0}{a\rho}},\qquad
 D_\infty=\frac{\alpha_0^{3/2}}{\sqrt{\rho a^3}},\qquad
 T_{\rm phys}=\frac{1}{4s_\infty^2D_\infty}>0.$$ These are fixed numbers depending on $a,\rho$, and the constants of the proof. In particular, $D_\infty>2D_*$.

Consider any sequence $L\to\infty$, $N/L^3\to\rho$. Choose $$K=\left\lfloor\sqrt{\frac{aN}{\alpha_0L}}\right\rfloor,
 \qquad s=L/K,\qquad D=N/K^3,\qquad r=a/s.$$ Then $K\to\infty$ and $$s\longrightarrow s_\infty,\qquad D\longrightarrow D_\infty,
 \qquad Dr=\frac{aN}{LK^2}\longrightarrow\alpha_0.$$ Thus the scaled core has the required form $r=\alpha/D$ with $\alpha\in[\alpha_0/2,2\alpha_0]$ eventually. Under the unitary dilation $x=s\widetilde x$, the Hamiltonian becomes $s^{-2}H_N$ on $\Lambda_K$, and the canonical Gibbs state has scaled temperature $T=s^2T_{\rm phys}$. Since $$DT=Ds^2T_{\rm phys}\longrightarrow\frac14,$$ we have $0<T\le D^{-1}$ eventually. The local uniformity of the volume threshold in Theorem <a href="#thm:scaled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:scaled">2</a> applies because $D$ remains in a compact neighborhood of $D_\infty$.

The dilation sends $u_{0,L}$ to $u_0$ and preserves the occupation fraction. Hence Theorem <a href="#thm:scaled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:scaled">2</a> gives, eventually along every such sequence, $$\frac{\langle u_{0,L},\gamma^{(1)}_{N,L,T_{\rm phys}}u_{0,L}\rangle}{N}
 \ge c_*.$$ Taking the lower limit proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.$\square$

```json
  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Two parts of the path construction, shown schematically. Left: the original dashed route meets the finite bad component $C$; the solid route joins its exterior predecessor $a$ to its exterior successor $b$ through good nearest-neighbor vertices. The drawing is a planar schematic of a three-dimensional exterior shell, and does not represent holes or later loop erasure. Right: for even $n$, the random transverse displacement used in the skeleton is reflected at $n/2$; the common linear drift and integer rounding are omitted. This return to zero fixes the final endpoint without a bridge conditioning.",
      "figure_id": "fig:lattice-detour",
      "pdf_page": 42,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:lattice-detour",
          "exact_quote": "Two parts of the path construction, shown schematically.\nLeft: the original dashed route meets the finite bad component $C$;\nthe solid route joins its exterior predecessor $a$ to its exterior\nsuccessor $b$ through good nearest-neighbor vert",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.62cm,y=0.62cm,font=\\small]\n \\begin{scope}\n  \\draw[step=1,gray!20,very thin] (0,0) grid (8,5);\n  \\foreach \\x/\\y in {3/2,3/3,4/2,4/3}\n   \\fill[gray!55] (\\x,\\y) circle (0.17);\n  \\draw[blue!65!black,densely dashed,line width=1.1pt]\n   (0,2)--(3,2)--(3,3)--(4,3)--(4,2)--(8,2);\n  \\draw[green!40!black,line width=1.4pt]\n   (0,2)--(2,2)--(2,1)--(5,1)--(5,2)--(8,2);\n  \\foreach \\x/\\y in {2/1,3/1,4/1,5/1,5/2,5/3,5/4,4/4,3/4,2/4,2/3,2/2}\n   \\draw[gray!65,fill=white] (\\x,\\y) circle (0.075);\n  \\fill (0,2) circle (0.09) node[left] {$v$};\n  \\fill (8,2) circle (0.09) node[right] {$w$};\n  \\node[above left] at (2,2) {$a$};\n  \\node[above right] at (5,2) {$b$};\n  \\node[above] at (3.5,3.1) {$C$};\n  \\node[align=center] at (4,-0.9) {replace the first-to-last visit};\n \\end{scope}\n \\begin{scope}[xshift=7cm]\n  \\draw[-{Latex[length=2mm]}] (0,0.4)--(6.2,0.4) node[right] {$t$};\n  \\draw[gray!55,dashed] (3,0.2)--(3,4.2);\n  \\draw[blue!65!black,line width=1.2pt]\n   (0,0.4)--(0.6,1.2)--(1.2,0.9)--(1.8,2.1)--(2.4,2.5)--(3,3.3);\n  \\draw[green!40!black,line width=1.2pt]\n   (3,3.3)--(3.6,2.5)--(4.2,2.1)--(4.8,0.9)--(5.4,1.2)--(6,0.4);\n  \\node[below] at (0,0.35) {$0$};\n  \\node[below] at (3,0.35) {$n/2$};\n  \\node[below] at (6,0.35) {$n$};\n  \\node at (3,4.6) {$Z_{\\min(t,n-t)}^{(j)}$};\n  \\node[align=center] at (3,-0.9) {read the same displacement twice};\n \\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Left: A schematic path routing. A horizontal dashed line segment connects an initial vertex $v$ through an exterior predecessor $a$, passes straight through a bad component region labeled $C$ enclosed by a dashed box, and reaches an exterior successor $b$, which continues to $w$. A solid green path runs from $v$ to $a$, detours around the region $C$ via multiple orthogonal segments through good nearest-neighbor vertices to reach $b$, and then continues to $w$. Below is the label \"replace the first-to-last visit\". Right: A coordinate plot with a horizontal axis labeled $t$, marked with ticks at $0$, $n/2$, and $n$. A curve begins at the origin $(0,0)$, rises to a peak labeled $Z_{\\min(t,n-t)}^{(j)}$ at $t = n/2$, and is reflected symmetrically back down to zero at $t = n$. Below is the label \"read the same displacement twice\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "The midpoint assignment for a route of three edges. A dashed column is one vertex factor with one common midpoint. Arrows identify the same particle label in the two copies; they are not trajectories in time. Removing the highlighted tags leaves the same two selected bath midpoints. All unselected bath paths and the configuration $W′$ are shared. For a route of arbitrary length the interior columns continue in the same way.",
      "figure_id": "cmp:shift-figure",
      "pdf_page": 47,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "cmp:shift-figure",
          "exact_quote": "The midpoint assignment for a route of three edges. A dashed\ncolumn is one vertex factor with one common midpoint. Arrows identify the\nsame particle label in the two copies; they are not trajectories in time.\nRemoving the highlighted tags l",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=2.25cm,y=1cm,>=Stealth,\n  every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n \\foreach \\x/\\txt in {0/{U_0=x},1/{U_1},2/{U_2},3/{U_3=y}} {\n   \\draw[gray!55,dashed,rounded corners]\n          (\\x-.41,.65) rectangle (\\x+.41,-1.55);\n   \\node[fill=white,inner sep=3pt] at (\\x,.65) {$\\txt$};\n }\n \\node[anchor=east] at (-.53,0) {first copy};\n \\node[anchor=east] at (-.53,-1.2) {second copy};\n \\node[draw,rounded corners,fill=blue!8] (a0) at (0,0) {$i_0$ (tag)};\n \\node[draw,rounded corners,fill=white] (a1) at (1,0) {$i_1$};\n \\node[draw,rounded corners,fill=white] (a2) at (2,0) {$i_2$};\n \\node at (3,0) {extra point};\n \\node at (0,-1.2) {extra point};\n \\node[draw,rounded corners,fill=white] (b1) at (1,-1.2) {$i_0$};\n \\node[draw,rounded corners,fill=white] (b2) at (2,-1.2) {$i_1$};\n \\node[draw,rounded corners,fill=blue!8] (b3) at (3,-1.2) {$i_2$ (tag)};\n \\draw[->,gray,thick] (a0.south east) -- (b1.north west);\n \\draw[->,gray,thick] (a1.south east) -- (b2.north west);\n \\draw[->,gray,thick] (a2.south east) -- (b3.north west);\n \\draw (.59,-1.7) -- (.59,-1.85) -- (2.41,-1.85) -- (2.41,-1.7);\n \\node at (1.5,-2.25) {same selected bath: $\\{U_1,U_2\\}$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A diagram with four columns and two rows of nodes, labeled on the left as \"first copy\" and \"second copy\". The columns are labeled at the top with midpoints: $U_0 = x$, $U_1$, $U_2$, $U_3 = y$. Each column is enclosed in a vertical dashed rounded rectangle indicating a vertex factor.\nIn the \"first copy\" row:\n- Column $U_0 = x$ contains an oval node labeled \"$i_0$ (tag)\".\n- Column $U_1$ contains an oval node labeled \"$i_1$\".\n- Column $U_2$ contains an oval node labeled \"$i_2$\".\n- Column $U_3 = y$ contains the text \"extra point\".\nIn the \"second copy\" row:\n- Column $U_0 = x$ contains the text \"extra point\".\n- Column $U_1$ contains an oval node labeled \"$i_0$\".\n- Column $U_2$ contains an oval node labeled \"$i_1$\".\n- Column $U_3 = y$ contains an oval node labeled \"$i_2$ (tag)\".\nThree curved directed arrows point from the first copy to the second copy, connecting matching labels: from $i_0$ (tag) to $i_0$, from $i_1$ to $i_1$, and from $i_2$ to $i_2$ (tag).\nAt the bottom, centered under columns $U_1$ and $U_2$, is the text: \"same selected bath: $\\{U_1, U_2\\}$\"."
    }
  ],
```

```json
      "explanation": "The thermal existence theorem proves macroscopic occupation of the constant orbital at strictly positive temperatures, generalizing the zero temperature ground state condensation theorem to canonical Gibbs states.",

      "source_anchor": "stmt-thm-scaled",
```
